European bank M&A is back with a 
bang 
European bank M&A has seen a resurgence in recent years, and 2027 should stay busy. 
Domestic deals still dominate, while cross-border consolidation is harder but is also 
picking up. Regulation is becoming more supportive, but politics and execution still 
tt  E t  d l  b t  t f ti  f E '  b ki  t  i  till

### Page 14

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

The rationale for European bank M&A has changed. After the financial crisis, deals were 
mostly about repair, followed by a wave of deals involving stronger domestic banks 
buying weaker rivals. Today, the backdrop has shifted – profitability and capital levels 
are stronger, making the obvious cost-efficiency story less compelling on its own. 
Instead, banks are using M&A to add scale, deposits and fee-generating diversification, 
while the need to close the gap with US rivals remains a key driver. 
The industrial logic has also strengthened, as fixed costs are rising in areas such as 
technology, AI, compliance, as well as personnel. Rising investment requirements mean 
that remaining subscale is becoming more expensive, even for highly profitable banks. 
Domestic dominates, but cross-border competes 
UniCredit-Commerzbank remains the sector’s most closely watched deal and could 
become the first major cross-border acquisition since the financial crisis. UniCredit has 
slowly been building its stake rather than a clean takeover and is now deemed to hold 
effective control, despite currently owning around 50% of the shares. Furthermore, it is 
also not entirely a leap into an unfamiliar market, as UniCredit already has a significant 
banking business in Germany through its subsidiary, HVB. 
The deal has also faced considerable pushback as well as political and stakeholder 
opposition, but is now looking increasingly likely to proceed. It has highlighted the 
difficulty of pulling off a large-scale cross-border deal for European banks. Yet it is now 
becoming the clearest live test of whether Europe is genuinely ready for cross-border 
bank consolidation. 
Domestic deals remain the rule, but we have seen other examples of cross-border deals. 
These include BPCE’s acquisition of Portuguese peer Novo Banco; Erste Bank's acquisition 
of Santander Bank Polska; and Crédit Agricole acquiring IKB to increase its presence in 
Germany. Some banks are also looking across the pond, such as Santander, which 
acquired Webster Financial Corporation in the US for USD12.5bn. 
These transactions are important, but they do not yet amount to a broad-based cross-
border consolidation wave. Many involve banks already familiar with the target market 
or assets that complement an existing local business.

### Page 15

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

Italy is still riding the wave 
Italy has become one of Europe’s busiest M&A markets, riding a new wave of 
consolidation, with Banca Monte dei Paschi (MPS) at the epicentre. The bank completed 
the acquisition of Mediobanca in 2025, and this year approached Banco BPM and Banca 
Generali for a merger as a counteroffensive to Intesa Sanpaolo’s hostile bid for MPS. 
The counteroffensive now appears less likely, as key shareholders have opposed parts of 
MPS’s merger plans and have also backed Intesa’s bid. The ECB has also provided 
authorisation to take control of MPS, representing an important step for the €34bn 
takeover. Alongside this, we had the BPER–Popolare di Sondrio merger earlier in the year, 
adding to Italy’s consolidation. 
Why is Italy so active in particular? Well, Italy remains quite fragmented (see below) and 
still offers a large domestic synergy pool, even after the wave of M&A that has already 
taken place. Furthermore, the country has meaningful industrial scale and offers further 
revenue diversification opportunities for banks and insurers. Although the obvious 
combinations are becoming less evident in Italy, we expect this to continue to drive M&A 
activity in 2027. 
Top 5 banks' lending market share by country (2025)

Source: AFME, ING

It's not only about getting bigger 
The deal mix is also broadening, as banks are increasingly focusing on fee income as 
well as scale, while also stepping into capital-light businesses. Deals in sectors such as 
wealth management, asset management, payments & FX and insurance are likely to 
feature prominently. 
NatWest Group’s acquisition of Evelyn Partners is a good example. The combination 
created the UK’s largest Private Banking and Wealth Management business, while lifting 
Natwest’s fee income by 20%. Similarly, BNP Paribas’s acquisition of AXA Investment 
Managers in 2025 created a €1.5tn asset management business, as the bank is seeking 
to compete with US asset management rivals and grow its fee income. In Germany, 
Deutsche Bank and DWS were reported to be exploring a potential minority investment 
in the life insurance business Frankfurter Leben. This would mark Deutsche’s return to 
life insurance after more than a decade. 
Consolidation can support bank performance 
But does consolidation actually deliver better outcomes? The evidence is not entirely 
clear-cut, but overall points in the right direction. While increasing scale is not always 
the best use of capital, in general, more consolidated markets tend to have more stable 
and, in some cases, higher profitability. The ECB is in favour of further consolidation 
where appropriate, as it sees the benefits of diversification and efficiency. On the other 
hand, capital can be reduced following a merger. This could be a downside, as better 
20%

### Page 16

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

capitalised banks tend to enjoy lower funding costs and thus have more room to lend to 
the real economy. 
The outcome therefore depends on the trade-off between longer-term synergies and 
the upfront capital, execution and integration costs. 
ECB data show an association between more concentrated banking markets and 
profitability. As the chart below shows, countries with a high level of concentration 
(defined by a higher Herfindahl-Hirschman Index coefficient) tend to have higher net 
interest margins on average. Still, net interest margins in Europe are structurally lower 
than in the US, where they tend to be between 3–4%. 
Net interest margin vs market concentration for European countries (2025)

Source: ECB, ING

Similarly, a higher level of market concentration in European countries is associated with 
a lower cost of risk. The correlation is modest, with some clear outliers, but the numbers 
show that greater concentration can lead to lower asset quality costs, although the 
relationship should perhaps not be interpreted as causal. Still, taken together, this 
suggests that consolidation can improve banking markets. 
Cost of risk vs market concentration for European countries (2025)

Source: ECB, ING

Regulation is helping, but can get in the way 
The Banking Union has made deals easier to assess: the ECB has become the single 
prudential supervisor for large banks, capital rules are more consistent, while resolution 
planning is now much clearer than it was even a decade ago. But as the Draghi report 
noted, the incomplete Banking Union means Europe’s banks remain less profitable than 
their US counterparts.

### Page 17

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

There is a shift underway, though. The EU Commission plans to table a broad Banking 
Package in the first quarter of 2027, with measures covering cross-border capital and 
liquidity management, deposit insurance, prudential rules and other items. For M&A, the 
most important proposals include those to give banking groups more flexibility to 
allocate capital and liquidity more efficiently within cross-border groups, while also 
applying more consistent treatment to intra-group exposures. These remain proposals 
rather than agreed implementations, meaning their final scope and timetable remain 
uncertain. 
The AFME estimates that regulatory ring-fencing rules mean that some €225bn of 
capital and €250bn of liquidity is trapped within subsidiaries, which banks cannot freely 
move between parent and subsidiaries. Freeing this up could also support more cross-
border transactions. 
Regulatory ring-fencing traps subsidiaries' capital and liquidity

Source: ECB, ING

The European Commission is also bringing forward a new proposal to replace the 
European Deposit Insurance Scheme (EDIS), which could reduce national ring-fencing 
incentives and encourage more cross-border M&A. CMDI reform has been passed this 
year, which could make it easier to deal with failing banks, potentially making 
consolidation easier as well, although the direct impact on M&A is unclear. 
The Danish compromise, which is now embedded into CRR3, allows banks to risk-weight 
certain insurance subsidiaries rather than deduct them from regulatory capital. This was 
clarified by the EBA in January 2026, and some banks such as UniCredit have received 
authorisation to use the Danish Compromise methodology for its insurance subsidiaries. 
This has improved the economics of bancassurance and could support further bank-
insurance transactions. 
On the other hand, national regulatory and political intervention has also put the brakes 
on M&A in some cases, raising execution costs and uncertainty. BBVA’s proposed 
acquisition of Sabadell in 2024 illustrates the problem. It took 539 days from the 
announcement in May 2024 to the offer failing in October 2025. 
For us, the message seems clear that European regulators are seeking to improve bank 
competitiveness, and they see consolidation as an important part of this. Looking 
ahead, regulation can improve the plumbing, but it is not a dealmaker. 
Why 2027 should stay busy 
The starting point for 2027 remains supportive, with bank profitability continuing to hold 
up well in 2026 while capital levels remain solid. While the geopolitical, inflation and 
interest rate backdrop poses headwinds, we still expect acquisition activity to remain 
high. On the other hand, management teams will increasingly need to choose between

€250bn
Capital Liquidity

### Page 18

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

retaining greater capital headroom in a less market-friendly environment, shareholder 
payouts, or M&A. 
The most likely deals are still domestic. Italy remains the M&A market of choice, but 
other geographies could also stand out. The Nordics are likely to see more partnerships 
and tech- and wealth-focused deals. Spain and Portugal are already quite concentrated, 
so we could see more bolt-ons. In the UK, challenger banks and neobanks are gaining 
ground while deposit competition remains intense, potentially leading to more activity. 
The Benelux has potential catalysts following a series of government ownership 
reductions and increasing inorganic ambitions. 
Cross-border activity could also remain elevated, but we think the real litmus test 
remains the UniCredit-Commerzbank deal, including what happens after completion in 
terms of execution and integration, a process that is likely to take a few years. The 
regulatory environment should also become increasingly more supportive for cross-
border M&A from 2027 onwards. 
Overall, 2027 could be another interesting year. 
M&A is here to stay 
European bank M&A has picked up in recent years, and we expect more activity again in 
2027, although we do not see a big bang. For investors, positioning is inherently 
speculative. For bondholders, the main way to gain is by owning the target entity's 
bonds. However, this is not always the case, and bigger doesn’t always mean better, as 
credit ratings don’t necessarily improve either following an acquisition or merger. 
We are not suggesting that a major change in the European banking landscape is 
imminent, as many obstacles remain, particularly with cross-border deals. But the 
direction of travel is interesting: larger banks often outperform smaller banks, while 
regulators continue to see benefits from a deeper, more integrated European banking 
union. Still, European banking markets remain fragmented to differing degrees, both 
between countries and compared to the US, presenting both opportunities and 
obstacles.

### Page 19

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

Bank capital enters 2027 on solid ground, while tight spreads and rising risks favour selectivity

AT1 reform remains on the back burner 
The European Commission published its report on competitiveness in the banking sector 
in July but did not include much in the way of proposals for AT1 bank capital 
instruments. The report mainly focused on reducing complexity, reporting requirements 
and improving competitiveness for Europe’s banking sector. 
For AT1, this probably means that the instruments are unlikely to be phased out 
anytime soon. What is somewhat relevant for AT1s is the Commission’s intention to 
reduce complexity for bank capital structures. This includes making the Pillar 2 
requirement and guidance more transparent, while the Commission also proposes to 
consolidate parts of the buffers into two buffers: one that can be released in periods of 
stress, and another that would be maintained in all scenarios. 
This, in theory, could reduce the risk of coupon skips, as being able to release buffers 
during periods of stress would not automatically lead to a breach of the combined buffer 
requirements. The proposals also include a removal of the leverage ratio Pillar 2 capital 
requirement, which would affect the largest European systemically important banks 
subject to this requirement. This is only a small number of banks but could (in theory) 
lead to a marginal decline in AT1 issuance. 
Overall, AT1 reform remains part of a broader discussion, with the EU Commission 
proposals also likely to change as negotiations remain ongoing and consensus needs to 
be built. For AT1, we expect the instruments to keep playing an important role (yay!) in 
European banks’ capital stacks, with no signs of an imminent abolition or grandfathering 
process. 
More supply while refinancing remains the main event 
With no major changes to AT1 structures or requirements imminent, we don’t expect a 
significant impact on banks’ capital instrument management and funding plans in 2027.

Jesse Norcross 
Senior Sector Strategist, Real Estate 
jesse.norcross@ing.com

Why AT1 still matters in 2027  
AT1 reform may remain on the back burner in 2027, leaving the instruments' role in bank 
capital largely unchanged. Solid fundamentals remain supportive, while supply should be 
manageable – but tight(ish) spreads and low reset levels could weigh on the market

### Page 20

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

Primary issuance in euro-denominated AT1 and Tier 2 has been in line with expectations 
in 2026. EUR AT1 issuance has just passed €15bn at the time of writing, slightly above 
our expectation of €15bn. Similarly, EUR Tier 2 has reached €27bn, nearing our 
expectation of €30bn for 2026. 
In 2027, we expect a modest increase in supply, largely dominated by the refinancing of 
upcoming redemptions/calls. We expect €20bn in AT1 supply, compared to €14bn of 
calls. While this represents a step-up, incremental net supply is only around €3bn higher 
than in 2026. Tier 2 redemptions will also rise by around €3bn next year, supporting our 
increased supply expectation of €35bn in Tier 2. 
While gross supply will be higher, net supply should remain easily absorbable. More 
supply should create opportunities in primary markets, although lower reset levels have 
left some recently issued bonds more exposed during weaker markets. 
AT1 redemptions pick up next year (€bn)

Source: IGM, ING

French banks: Déjà vu? 
French sovereign risk has increased, with the 10-year OAT-Bund spread now trading at 
around 160bp at the time of writing, the highest level since 2011. The French 10y yield is 
around 5%, the highest level since 2002. The story in French markets is growing more 
sinister by the day. But contagion risk across the eurozone is (so far) relatively 
contained. Italy and Belgium stand out as other higher-risk countries, given high debt 
levels and deficits, but it remains a French story for now. Nonetheless, the domestic 
sovereign exposure and the sovereign-bank nexus have come back into play. 
So far, the market is not questioning the broader future of the eurozone. Still, France is of 
a much larger magnitude than countries such as Greece or even Italy, which had to be 
supported during the eurozone crisis. 
What can be said is that French banks, as well as the broader European banking sector, 
are in much better shape than they were during the eurozone crisis. French banks’ 
capital ratios have increased significantly and now stand at around 13-15% on average, 
compared to c.9-10% during the eurozone crisis. Liquidity levels are healthy, while 
earnings and exposures, particularly for the larger banks, are well diversified. For 
instance, BNP Paribas generated around 25% of its revenues in 2025 in France, while 
French deposits account for 29% of the total. For Société Générale, France accounts for 
40% of exposure. 
French banks’ direct exposure to OATs is also not that large; it represents around 6% of 
total assets, while roughly two-thirds is held at amortised cost, making holdings less 
sensitive to valuation declines. Some French banks do carry higher exposure, particularly 
those with limited geographic diversification. For instance, La Banque Postale shows the

### Page 21

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

highest OAT exposure as a percentage of CET1, at 114%. But around 95% is held at 
amortised cost. 
On average, for most French banks, OAT holdings represent around 15-30% of CET1. For 
context, the data suggests that a 100bp widening of the large French banks’ domestic 
sovereign holdings would lower CET1 for BNP and Société Générale by less than 10bp, 
and up to 25bp for Crédit Agricole. 
French banks OAT exposure appears manageable

Source: Company reports, EBA, ING

Still, the risks are rising and sustained high OAT-Bund levels could feed into the real 
economy, credit demand, confidence and asset quality. While non-performing loans 
(NPLs) are low for French banks, they have risen a bit in recent quarters, and we could 
see more procyclical provisioning from French banks, in particular for SMEs and 
consumer loans. 
Risks could also arise from other avenues. Moody’s surprisingly maintained France’s 
rating at Aa3, with a negative outlook, despite broad expectations of a downgrade. 
Nonetheless, rating downgrades remain a risk, and S&P is due for review at the end of 
November, bringing further sovereign ratings pressure. 
A larger move in OATs would also be more disruptive, as higher yields, social unrest and 
political fragmentation complicate deficit reduction. October’s preliminary composite 
PMI of 48.4 adds to the weaker-growth picture. 
However, we do not think this is the same sovereign-bank loop as in 2009-2012. 
European Central Bank funding facilities, the Bank Recovery and Resolution Directive 
(BRRD) and stronger liquidity coverage ratio (LCR) and net stable funding ratio (NSFR) 
requirements have reduced banks’ dependence on sovereign guarantees. The link 
remains: ratings, domestic bond holdings and a shared operating environment still 
transmit sovereign stress into bank funding costs and market access. 
The flip side is that liquidity requirements and favourable EU sovereign risk weights have 
made banks important buyers of government debt. As ECB purchases ended and TLTROs 
were repaid, sovereign holdings have partly replaced central bank reserves for LCR 
purposes. Banks rely less on sovereign guarantees, but on the other hand, sovereigns 
arguably rely more on banks for funding. 
This new relationship, alongside expectations that the ECB would contain broader 
spillovers, perhaps helps explain why bank spreads have not widened as much as OATs. 
The ECB’s refinancing and bond purchase tools remain part of the toolkit, although they 
have previously been used in a different inflation and rates environment.

Without sovereign relief, pressure is likely to persist, particularly for domestically 
focused banks with larger relative OAT holdings. Limited issuance may slow the 
repricing, but the distinction between issuers remains important, especially in AT1. 
French AT1 underperforms 
French AT1 has underperformed YTD and now trades wider than most other major 
country cohorts in EUR AT1. That mostly reflects heightened sovereign-bank nexus 
concerns and OAT-Bund spread widening, rather than a deterioration in fundamentals. 
However, it also reflects market concerns about potential ratings impacts and ongoing 
political and fiscal volatility, as well as a weakening in the macro environment. 
We would avoid treating all French banks similarly. Sensitivity to OATs and the French 
economy differ quite a bit across issuers. As such, the recent widening has been quite 
uniform, but the market could become more selective in French AT1s if nervousness 
continues into 2027. We think the most diversified names, including BNP and ACAFP in 
2027 and 2028 callable bonds, with high reset levels, should hold up better. 
French AT1 spreads trade at the widest levels in EUR

Source: IHS Markit, ING

Calling remains the base case, while the economics are changing 
Banks have continued to call their AT1s this year, and calls (or tenders) remain our base 
case for 2027. While the decision to call or extend is highly dependent on market 
circumstances and, to a lesser extent, driven by regulatory approval, we still see a high 
bar for not calling/replacing. 
AT1 reset levels have hit lows in 2026

Source: IHS Markit, ING

This is especially the case as the reset levels remain low, while overall coupon levels are 
in line with recent historical averages. The economics become less comfortable further

AT1 and Tier 2: Spread widening at different speeds 
The EUR AT1 index has widened by 19bp year-to-date, outperforming callable and bullet 
Tier 2. Over the last three months, the picture looks different, though. AT1 has 
underperformed Tier 2 as recent market stress has led to quite a bit of widening. On a 
beta-adjusted basis, AT1 has had a mixed performance over three months but has 
outperformed on a YTD basis, suggesting the recent sell-off has reversed the trend a 
little bit 
AT1 has widened in recent months as volatility has come back to markets

Source: IHS Markit, ING

However, the correction has only moved spreads from tight to merely quite tight. The 
AT1 index now trades at a similar level to the 2y median of asw+295bp. Callable Tier 2 
also trades just inside the 2y median after the recent widening. The AT1-Callable Tier 2 
differential is around asw+150bp, while the AT1/Callable Tier 2 ratio is also in line with 
the 2-year average. 
Both remain quite compressed compared to historical levels, suggesting that on spread 
alone, Tier 2 currently offers better value. We see somewhat greater widening risk in 
2027, as bank fundamentals soften at the margin, while supply rises a bit and entry 
valuations remain slightly compressed. 
This does not make AT1 too rich, but the yield angle remains the key driver of why AT1 
could continue to perform well. With rates likely to remain elevated, additional yield 
combined with some spread widening may lead investors to wait for better entry points.

### Page 24

Reset has mattered more than ratings 
High reset spread AT1s have generally been more resilient in 2026, with the 700-800bp 
reset bucket even bucking the trend with some tightening over the last three months. 
While this relationship is not fully linear, and many high-reset bonds are issued by 
higher beta/lower rated issuers, numbers still suggest that reset levels have been an 
important driver of performance this year. Higher underlying rates reduce the cushion 
that lower reset AT1 bonds have had, as extension risk concerns have made something 
of a comeback in recent months. 
Higher reset levels also provide greater coupon protection if a bond is extended during a 
period of market stress. There are a few near-term low-reset bonds callable with reset 
levels below +380bp. However, extension risk is still low for these: longer-call AT1s from 
these issuers trade through the respective reset levels, and these banks have historically 
assessed calls holistically, rather than purely on an economics or bond-by-bond basis. 
Higher-rated bonds have underperformed in 2026. IG-rated bonds have not translated 
into better spread performance: BBB- rated AT1s now trade at similar spread levels as 
BB+ and BB-rated AT1s. 
Reset levels have been a better guide for performance than credit ratings in 2026 
High reset AT1s have been more resilient...  … while higher rated bonds have seen less love

Source: IHS Markit, ING  Source: IHS Markit, ING

So where does that leave the AT1 market in 2027? 
2027 is shaping up to be another interesting year for bank capital. Valuations look a 
bit compressed, but bank fundamentals remain solid, with banks likely to continue 
calling their instruments at the first call dates. In terms of supply, we expect the 
small increase to be absorbable but potentially lead to investors becoming a bit 
more price sensitive. Furthermore, the lack of imminent AT1 reform should mean 
the asset class retains an important role in Europe’s banking capital stacks for the 
foreseeable future. 
There are risks becoming more visible, not least through sovereign and fiscal 
concerns, with France the obvious victim at the moment. A softening of 
fundamentals at the margin and lower reset levels further along the call curve could 
create more volatility. Spreads have recently seen some widening, yet there is 
probably greater scope for spreads to stay wider than for further tightening in 2027. 
AT1 yields could remain a good driver of demand for investors, cushioning potential 
spread weakness. On the other hand, the market is likely to differentiate more 
between structures and issuer profiles, while remaining wary of banks with greater 
domestic sovereign sensitivity.

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

Stablecoins are increasingly moving into the mainstream financial system as banks assess both the risks and 
opportunities they present

Stablecoins’ market capitalisation has grown significantly over the past two years. This 
growth has drawn widespread scrutiny, including from regulators, as it raises concerns 
about financial stability. Banks have also paid closer attention to the sector as it could 
represent both new risks and opportunities. 
In this piece, we review the potential impact stablecoins could have on the European 
banking sector. This is especially relevant as multiple regulatory changes are expected in 
2027. The EU is reviewing its Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation (MiCAR), which could 
affect the estimated impact of stablecoins on the banking sector. Across the Atlantic, 
the regulatory landscape could also change next year. Discussions could result in the US 
allowing stablecoin issuers to directly distribute rewards, which would be a first. 
Overall, while the future of stablecoins is still uncertain, they promise to remain in the 
spotlight in 2027. We show that despite multiple risks identified, European banks are 
unlikely to surrender ground to fintech stablecoin issuers without a fight. 
What are stablecoins? 
Stablecoins are crypto assets issued by private institutions that promise a stable 
nominal value in a given currency, commodity or pool of assets. Stablecoins are 
therefore not issued (or backed) by a government or central authority, but rather by a 
private institution and secured by segregated reserves. 
The crypto asset is issued on distributed ledger technology (DLT), such as a blockchain, 
with three existing types of pegging mechanisms. For the sake of this analysis, we will 
focus on the main one: fiat-collateralised stablecoins. These crypto assets are backed 
1:1 by reserves of cash or liquid assets. 
In our overview publication on stablecoins (read more here), we review the sector’s 
recent development. We show that the total market capitalisation of stablecoins is

Marine Leleux 
Sector Strategist, Financials 
marine.leleux2@ing.com

Stablecoins’ growth – opportunity or 
threat for banks? 
While stablecoin adoption could significantly affect European banks, including by shifting 
their funding base, the sector could also benefit from the technology by issuing its own 
tokens. Upcoming regulatory changes could alter the impact of stablecoins on banks, but 
we should not expect banks to surrender ground to fintech issuers without a fight

### Page 26

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

surpassing $300bn globally, 99% of which consists of USD-pegged tokens. While still 
dwarfed by, for example, bank deposits, the rapid growth of the sector over the past five 
years (up by US$250bn) has drawn a lot of attention. 
Selected stablecoins' market capitalisation over time & share of all cryptocurrencies

USDT = Tether, USDC = Circle, USDS = Sky Protocol  
Source: ING Research, IMF

Growing interest in stablecoins stems both from possible efficiency gains and the 
potential risks they pose. For stablecoins to reach the ‘broad adoption’ level of 
development, usage should extend beyond the niche crypto landscape and into the 
payments world. Several variables, including regulation, integration into payment 
systems and relative attractiveness compared with other payment systems, must be 
considered when determining the future growth of stablecoins. Read more on that in 
our piece Stablecoins 101: Uncertain growth but the stakes are high. 
Despite their uncertain future, we believe it's important to consider a scenario in which 
stablecoins become broadly adopted and systemically important as a means of 
payment. We identify multiple potential impacts of the crypto asset, including 
implications for emerging markets, interest rates, and monetary policy. In this piece, we 
will focus specifically on potential implications for the banking sector. 
Stablecoins impact on banks: Threat, opportunity, or both? 
In this section, we explore the potential implications of a broad use of stablecoins 
beyond the crypto landscape and into payment systems for banks. Despite US dollar 
stablecoins currently dominating the market, our main assumption is the widespread 
adoption of euro-denominated stablecoins in Europe. 
While stablecoins are often presented as a challenge for the banking sector, we review 
here five potential implications, not all of which are negative for financial institutions. 
A change in banks' funding 
To discuss the consequences of an uptake in stablecoin adoption on banks’ funding, two 
variables must be considered: 
1. Which assets are used to buy stablecoins 
2. What type of reserves are required by the regulation 
If most users acquire stablecoins by redeeming liquid investments, the impact for 
financial institutions is expected to remain limited. However, if EU citizens and 
corporates adopt EUR-denominated stablecoins using bank deposits, the impact on 
financial institutions could be substantially more significant. In this scenario, banks could 
face a net drop in their retail and corporate deposits and/or a shift in the composition of 
their funding base. 
1

### Page 27

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

Importantly, the nature of banks’ funding change depends on the second key variable: 
the regulation in place, more specifically the reserve regime. Indeed, widespread 
adoption of the crypto asset with deposits could result in a drop in banks’ funding. 
However, under the EU’s MiCAR, stablecoin issuers are required to hold part of their 
reserves as deposits with European commercial banks. 
This implies that part of the amount flowing out of banks would ultimately come back to 
the sector as reserves from stablecoin issuers. Stablecoin adoption would therefore not 
translate into a net drop in funding for banks but rather in a shift in their funding base, 
where retail and corporate deposits would come back in wholesale form (more 
specifically as financial institution deposits). 
Illustration of banks' funding shift through stablecoins adoption using retail deposits*

*stylized example using MiCAR for systemic issuers 
Source: ING

This shift from retail to wholesale deposits is not without consequences. Wholesale 
deposits are known to be more volatile than retail ones and therefore represent a less 
stable funding source for banks. Additionally, capital requirements are higher for 
wholesale deposits, with, for example, higher run-off rates in the Liquidity Coverage 
Ratio (LCR) and lower available stable funding weights in the Net Stable Funding Ratio 
(NSFR). This reflects their greater volatility and related risks, making them more 
expensive for banks to hold. 
Greater reliance on wholesale deposits could also create risk management challenges 
with more complex asset-liability matching. The ease with which deposits can be 
switched into stablecoins could increase financial institutions’ exposure to economic 
cycles. 
To date, all main regulations in place ban stablecoin issuers from directly remunerating 
holders. Therefore, an environment in which interest rates are lowered (or even 
approach zero) would make holding stablecoins more attractive for users, as the 
difference with holding a deposit account is reduced. This could trigger sudden 
stablecoin adoption and a quick shift in banks’ funding. 
The opposite scenario could also pose challenges for banks. As interest rates increase, 
the attractiveness of holding stablecoins diminishes. This could lead to sudden 
redemption requests from stablecoin holders, forcing issuers to liquidate reserves held at 
banks. As a result, financial institutions could face an outflow of stablecoin issuers’ 
deposits with a simultaneous inflow of retail deposits, adding volatility to their funding 
profiles. 
In either case, these funding changes and substitution dynamics could make banks’ 
funding management more complex, costly and difficult to predict. However, as long

### Page 28

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

as regulation prevents stablecoin issuers from directly remunerating holders, the effect 
of economic cycles is expected to remain muted, as stablecoins only become attractive 
as a deposit-like instrument in the case of a very low interest rate environment. 
Additionally, the impact is likely to vary across the banking sector. Financial institutions 
with a large base of digitally active customers could face heightened pressure from 
widespread stablecoin adoption, as the barriers to switching from deposits to crypto 
assets are lower. Differences could also emerge between deposit categories. The impact 
is expected to be especially noticeable for accounts used as payment instruments, held 
by younger, digitally native customers who tend to be less relationship-driven or loyal to 
a specific bank and generally more willing to adopt new financial technologies. 
While these points explore a scenario in which EUR-denominated stablecoins are 
widespread, it’s important to note that the impact would be different if USD-
denominated stablecoins continue to dominate the market. In that case, a significant 
uptake would not translate into a funding shift for European banks but rather into a net 
outflow of deposits. 
Indeed, these funds could flow to US stablecoin issuers, which would place the safe 
assets as required under US regulation. In other words, this would translate into a net 
outflow of European deposits towards the US economy. Aside from the monetary policy 
impact, European banks would be required to turn to alternative and more expensive 
sources of funding to compensate for the outflow of retail deposits. 
However, EU banks can also play a central role in the issuance and adoption of EUR-
denominated stablecoins to counter that effect, as we discuss below. 
Pressure on credit provision 
In addition to changing banks’ funding base, broad stablecoin adoption could also 
negatively impact credit provision through several channels. 
In a scenario where banks see a funding shift towards greater reliance on wholesale 
deposits, higher liquidity requirements may push them to take more conservative risk 
decisions. Research has shown that this could translate into banks steering their balance 
sheets away from illiquid and long-maturity loans to ensure they comply with the 
regulation, as well as reducing loan volumes, shortening maturities and increasing loan 
pricing. Overall, the European Central Bank has shown that deposit substitution could 
lead to a contraction of about 0.2% in lending to firms. 
In addition to regulatory restrictions, wholesale deposits show different volatility 
patterns from those of retail deposits, mainly by being more reactive to market and 
macroeconomic changes. Greater reliance on such deposits would, once again, imply 
more complex risk management, pushing banks to hold additional liquidity buffers 
which could increase loan pricing. 
Here, again, the impact could differ depending on the financial institution. European 
banks already showing high loan-to-deposit ratios, as well as those close to regulatory 
thresholds, could be more inclined to pass higher funding costs on through lending 
conditions in order to remain within regulatory thresholds. 
Concentration and spillover risks 
A growing discussion among regulators centres on concentration risks. As stablecoin 
issuers are required to hold part of their reserves as deposits with commercial banks, 
financial institutions could increasingly rely on their deposits for funding. As the 
proportion of banks’ funding coming from a small number of stablecoin issuers 
increases, so does concentration risk. This impact may become substantial if stablecoin 
adoption is both significant and concentrated among a handful of issuers. 
2 
3

### Page 29

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

Concentration could also arise if stablecoin issuers maintain relationships with only a 
limited number of banks, those with both the technical capabilities and willingness to 
engage with the sector. This could create pockets of concentration on both sides of the 
business, introducing new systemic risks for the banking sector. 
Regulators across the globe have been increasingly attentive to the development of 
stablecoins. However, as concentration risks between banks and stablecoin issuers 
potentially emerge, they might also see the need to update their frameworks to ensure 
that such risks are correctly accounted for and captured in stress testing. 
In addition, the current EU regulation also creates a direct link between the banking 
sector and crypto asset issuers, increasing spillover risks. In the event of a run on 
stablecoins, where the crypto asset suddenly loses value and users redeem their 
holdings, banks carrying the issuer’s reserves could face sudden and significant 
redemption requests. This could transmit a shock in the crypto sector to the broader 
financial system. The reverse is also true, with stablecoin issuers potentially affected by 
the failure of, or reputational damage to, the commercial bank they hold reserves with. 
These concerns promise to remain relevant in 2027 as the EU is reviewing its MiCA 
Regulation. In May 2026, the European Commission opened a consultation to assess 
whether the MiCAR remains ‘fit for purpose’ following its initial implementation and 
recent market developments. In September, the ECB, alongside the 27 EU national 
central banks, submitted a recommendation to drop the requirement for stablecoin 
issuers to hold deposits at commercial banks. 
Instead, the European System of Central Banks proposes implementing a minimum 
percentage of assets maturing within one to five working days to be held by the 
stablecoin issuer. This is said to lower both concentration and spillover risks. However, it 
could further accentuate the impact on banks’ funding by transforming a funding shift 
into a net deposit outflow. 
Stablecoin and, more generally, crypto asset regulations are still emerging globally. 
Other regulators have highlighted the risks that links between stablecoins and the 
banking sector could create. The most striking example comes from the UK, where the 
Bank of England announced that it aims to ban stablecoin issuers from holding reserves 
at commercial banks, specifically to mitigate spillover risks in case of a shock. As 
stablecoin usage continues to rise, we can expect further changes to the regulatory 
landscape globally. 
Competing through innovation 
Despite the lack of international harmonisation, current regulations restrict stablecoin 
issuers from directly distributing remuneration to holders. This represents a significant 
regulatory advantage for the banking sector, as it limits the use of the crypto asset to 
transactional purposes. Remuneration distribution is therefore a central discussion point 
for stablecoin issuers, as removing the ban would allow them to position their product 
not only as a means of payment but also as a store of value. 2027 will be a crucial year 
as negotiations surrounding the US Clarity Act continue, potentially allowing stablecoin 
issuers to directly distribute rewards. While still far from an agreement, such a 
derogation would be a first globally and could open the door for similar changes in other 
jurisdictions. To counter this competition, banks could increase interest rates on their 
deposit accounts; however, this would have an impact on their profitability. 
Therefore, the critical response to the rise of stablecoins lies instead in innovation. 
Indeed, crypto assets’ main advantage lies in their speed and limited costs, but banks 
are already showing they can innovate to compete. The European payment landscape 
is relatively cheap and fast despite being heavily fragmented. Some national payment 
systems exist, but a large share of transactions are processed through non-EU providers. 
4

### Page 30

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

Stablecoins’ promise to process payments (nearly) instantly and at extremely low costs, 
even across borders, could put pressure on existing systems. However, banks and 
payment system providers are already exploring ways to compete and experimenting 
with new digital transaction methods. 
One example lies in tokenisation. Tokenised assets are digital representations of real-
world assets on a distributed ledger infrastructure. A wide range of assets can be 
tokenised, but most banks are experimenting with deposit tokenisation. These could 
modernise payment and settlement infrastructures as well as intragroup liquidity 
management. They promise faster settlement, 24/7 transactions, programmability and 
lower operational frictions. Additionally, tokenised deposits offer regulatory advantages, 
as they remain fully within the existing supervisory framework. The underlying assets 
stay on the commercial banks’ balance sheet, preserving the same oversight and 
prudential standards applied to traditional deposits. 
Banks’ competitive response could also extend to the regulatory field. The sector could 
push regulators to implement or maintain strict rules for the crypto asset sector to 
adequately reflect their risks. The US provides us with a striking example, as negotiations 
for the Clarity Act have been ongoing for months, with financial institutions pressing the 
importance of the ‘same risk, same regulation’ principle. While the final version of the US 
crypto landscape regulation is still to be agreed upon, US banks have played a central 
role in the negotiations by requesting proportionate safeguards surrounding the use of 
crypto assets. 
Banks' role in stablecoin adoption 
While we have explored in depth the impact stablecoins could have on the banking 
sector upon broad adoption, it’s important to highlight that banks also play a role in 
the crypto asset’s adoption. A central variable in determining the future adoption and 
development of stablecoins is the degree to which they are integrated into existing 
payment systems. Greater integration not only increases accessibility for a broader 
range of customers but also improves the ease of use. This is especially relevant as 
stablecoins are still mainly used in the crypto landscape and by digitally savvy users. 
Banks could therefore support the adoption of stablecoins for retail, day-to-day 
payments by integrating the asset into their digital systems or creating their own 
tokens, adding it as a new payment rail. Several examples in the EU and the US show 
the role banks can play in issuing their own stablecoins, defending their place in the 
payment landscape, even when it comes to new technologies. By issuing their own 
stablecoins, banks could also avoid losing client relationships and reduce the risk of 
becoming commodity providers for new payment issuers. Beyond this, banks are also 
exploring ways in which stablecoin integration could improve the customer experience 
while increasing fee revenues. 
Looking specifically at Europe, banks’ stablecoin issuance could also play a significant 
role in the growth of EUR-denominated stablecoins. To date, USD-pegged stablecoins 
represent a striking 99% of all stablecoins in use. The widespread use of USD stablecoins 
in the EU would have significant monetary implications on top of those discussed here 
for the banking sector. Over the past few years, the European Union has increasingly 
focused on its ‘strategic autonomy’ agenda. Part of the discussion concerns the Union’s 
significant reliance on non-EU payment services, primarily US-based ones. The 
development of EUR-denominated stablecoins by local banks could provide an answer 
to these concerns, allowing for the development of a fast, cost-efficient European 
payment system directly competing with non-EU alternatives. 
5

### Page 31

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

Potentially significant implications, but banks are unlikely to 
surrender ground to fintech stablecoin issuers 
The broad adoption of stablecoins could have a significant impact on European banks. 
These range from a net drop in retail deposits or a shift towards wholesale funding to 
greater concentration and spillover risks, as well as an overall negative effect on credit 
provision and potentially banks’ business models. 
While it remains important to continue exploring these risks as stablecoin use grows, it’s 
also crucial to highlight that the banking sector has tools at its disposal to not only 
counter them, but also benefit from the underlying technology. Indeed, one should not 
expect the “traditional” financial sector to simply stand aside as crypto assets continue 
to gain traction. 
Despite significant growth over the past five years, stablecoins remain far from reaching 
widespread adoption. In our previous publication (read more here), we identified several 
factors that could shape their future. For banks, the key question is how to compete with 
the benefits stablecoins offer. The sector is already making substantial progress in 
instant-payment technologies and exploring digital alternatives (such as tokenisation) 
that could provide similar benefits with fewer regulatory concerns. These initiatives 
could significantly reduce stablecoins’ relative appeal. Furthermore, it’s essential to 
remember that banks still play a central role in payments and could also play a crucial 
role in the uptake of stablecoins by issuing their own tokens. This includes influencing 
which currency-pegged stablecoins gain traction and how they are integrated into the 
broader financial system. 
Next year promises to deliver another wave of stablecoin-related news. In the EU, the 
MiCAR review could translate into a change in the reserve framework, a key issue when 
assessing the potential impact of stablecoins on banks. In the UK, 2027 will also be an 
important year as it will see the enforcement of its comprehensive stablecoin regime in 
October. The US must also still finalise its CLARITY Act, potentially allowing stablecoin 
issuers to directly distribute remuneration to holders. This would be a world first and a 
major shift in the regulatory treatment of the crypto asset, opening the door to similar 
changes in other countries. 
While the upcoming regulatory changes could alter the potential impact of stablecoin 
adoption on the banking sector, we should not expect banks to surrender ground to 
fintech stablecoin issuers without a fight.

### Page 32

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

On 30 January 2027, the amendments to the Solvency II regime will apply, making 
these revisions one of the important regulatory themes for 2027. One of the key 
objectives of the Solvency II reforms is to release capital and promote long-term 
investments by European insurance companies in support of the ambitions of Europe’s 
Savings and Investments Union (SIU). Such capital release could also increase the 
capacity of insurance companies to invest more in corporate and bank bonds. 
During the past decade, insurance companies have reduced their bond holdings in 
favour of equity. Rather than an active reallocation of assets, this partially reflects 
second-round effects related to the strong equity market growth and the rise in bond 
yields and corresponding decline in bond valuations against the backdrop of the ECB 
rate hikes in 2022 and 2023. Higher yields have also coincided in recent years with a 
stronger focus by banks on issuance in shorter maturities, rather than the longer 
maturities traditionally favoured by insurance companies. This has been particularly 
noteworthy for covered bonds. 
We don’t expect the Solvency II revisions to reverse this trend, particularly as the 
amendments promote long-term equity investments. Instead, for most bank bonds, the 
spread-risk capital treatment remains broadly unchanged. Unsecured and subordinated 
bank bonds continue to be treated as corporate bonds with capital charges determined 
by their ratings and duration, while qualifying covered bonds keep their preferential 
treatment. The impact of the Solvency II revisions therefore arises mainly indirectly 
through lower overall capital requirements, while securitisations are the only debt 
instruments benefiting directly through a more favourable capital treatment. 
Nonetheless, certain reforms are worth highlighting for their potential impact on 
insurance demand for different bank bonds. Among these, the amendments relating to

bank bonds. Securitisations benefit most directly through lower capital charges, while 
covered bonds could benefit modestly from the new liquidity-buffer rules

long-term equity investments, securitisations and the volatility adjustment are 
particularly noteworthy. 
Insurance companies increase equity versus debt exposures (€bn)

Source: EIOPA, ING

Long-term equity investments and non-life liquidity buffers 
Under Solvency II, insurance companies can classify part of their equity holdings as 
long-term equity investments (LTEI), benefiting from a lower 22% capital charge. To 
qualify, these investments must be held for at least five years, be listed in the EEA or 
OECD, meet diversification standards and be managed under a long-term investment 
policy. The insurer must also be able to avoid forced sales for five years, even under 
stressed conditions. 
The Solvency II amended Delegated Regulation introduces two approaches to 
demonstrate that forced equity sales can be avoided. 1) The insurer must either have 
sufficient illiquid homogeneous life insurance liabilities with a duration of more than 9.5 
years or a liquidity buffer exceeding 100% for non-life insurance obligations. 2) The 
insurer can also apply a forced-selling test to prove that it can generate cash inflows 
that are consistently higher than the cash outflows over five years, including under 
stress. 
The liquidity buffer requirements for non-life insurance activities are similar to the 
Liquidity Coverage Ratio (LCR) requirements for banks. The portfolio of liquid assets can 
comprise Level 1, Level 2A and Level 2B assets, with the total value of Level 2A and Level 
2B assets capped at 40% and the value of the Level 2B assets at 15%. 
• Qualifying Level 1 assets, subject to a 0% haircut, are cash, exposures to the ECB, 
multilateral development banks and international organisations, and domestic 
currency exposures to EEA central governments and central banks. 
• Qualifying Level 2A assets, subject to a 15% haircut, are bonds and loans rated AA- 
or better that are not issued by an insurance company or bank, and covered bonds 
rated AA- or better as referred to in Article 3(1) of the European Covered Bond 
Directive (CBD) (ie ‘European Covered Bonds’). 
• Qualifying Level 2B assets, subject to a 25% haircut, are Simple, Transparent and 
Standardised (STS) securitisations (not originated by group entities) rated AA- or 
better or that constitute a senior tranche; or subject to a 50% haircut, bonds and 
loans rated A+ to BBB- that are not issued by an insurance company or bank, and 
equity investments listed in the EEA or OECD, or traded on multilateral trading 
facilities, that are not long-term or strategic equity investments and are not 
investments in an insurance company or bank.

While unsecured bank bonds are excluded as eligible liquid assets, the LTEI-related 
liquidity buffer approach could generate some additional demand for covered bonds or 
STS securitisations of banks. However, we don’t expect this effect to be significant given 
the relatively small share of non-life insurance obligations and insurers’ sizeable existing 
portfolios of other liquid assets, such as government bonds. 
Insurers have limited investments in covered bonds or collateralised securities

Source: EIOPA, ING

The LTEI liquidity approach is separate from the generic liquidity and concentration risk 
management requirements under Solvency II, as well as from the obligation under the 
amended Solvency II Directive to provide for a liquidity risk management plan. 
Lower capital requirements for securitisations 
The amended Solvency II Delegated Regulation lowers the capital requirements for 
securitisation notes. This should encourage insurers to invest more in these transactions, 
helping banks to transfer risk and expand lending. 
• The risk factors (ai and bi) for the spread risk of senior tranches of STS securitisations 
will become fully aligned with the treatment of similarly rated covered bonds as 
referred to in CBD Article 3(1). 
• The risk factors for non-senior STS securitisations are reduced to a similar extent, 
with the new capital charges for AAA-rated non-senior notes at c.70% of the current 
capital charge, and for AA-rated non-senior notes at 75% of the current requirement. 
• The difference in the capital treatment between STS and non-STS securitisations is 
currently far more significant for insurers than for banks. This difference will be 
reduced substantially, while the capital charge remains capped at 100%. 
• The current Solvency II regime makes no distinction between senior and non-senior 
non-STS securitisation notes, exaggerating the spread risk of senior compared with 
non-senior tranches. Hence, lower risk factors are introduced for senior non-STS 
compared to non-senior non-STS transactions. 
As a result of these amendments, the difference in capital requirements between CBD-
compliant covered bonds and senior STS securitisations will disappear from the end of 
January, while in the highest rating buckets (AAA and AA) securitisations will be treated 
better than unsecured bonds or third-country covered bonds. Further down the rating 
scale, STS securitisations will be treated comparably to unsecured bonds from a capital 
perspective, where corporate and bank unsecured bonds now benefit from a more 
favourable capital charge compared to similarly rated senior STS securitisations.

European insurance companies currently hold only about €37bn in collateralised debt, 
such as asset-backed securities (ABS) and residential mortgage-backed securities 
(RMBS). This is only 12% of what they hold in covered bonds and 3% of their corporate 
debt portfolio. 
The Solvency II amendments may motivate insurance companies to diversify their debt 
portfolios a bit further away from covered bonds and other bank bond products towards 
securitisations. However, the impact is not expected to be substantial given that the 
weighted average lives of securitisation notes may not always be well suited to the 
longer-term investment needs of insurance companies. 
Volatility adjustment to align spread sensitivities more closely 
Insurance companies calculate the best estimate of their liabilities by discounting the 
expected future liability cash flows using the relevant risk-free interest rate term 
structure. To mitigate the balance sheet impact of bond spread volatility (ie, on the 
asset side of their balance sheet), insurance companies can discount their liabilities at 
the risk-free rate plus a volatility adjustment (VA).

The volatility adjustment increases the discount rate 
Spot rate, %

Source: EIOPA, ING

For each relevant currency (or national market in the case of the euro area), the 
volatility adjustment represents 65% of the spread (S) between the interest rate on the 
reference portfolio and the relevant risk-free interest rate, adjusted for a risk correction 
(RC). The latter correction factor removes the part of the spread that is attributable to 
expected losses or unexpected credit or other risks to the assets. The volatility 
3

### Page 36

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

adjustment is only added to the applicable risk-free interest rates that are not derived 
from extrapolation. 
For each relevant country, a country-specific adjustment may also be added to the 
volatility adjustment before the application of the 65% factor if a) the risk-corrected 
country spread is more than twice the risk-corrected currency spread, and b) the risk-
corrected country spread is more than 85bp. The country component in the volatility 
adjustment helps mitigate excessive widening in specific countries. 
Under the current regime, the representative currency and country portfolios are 
constructed as uniform portfolios, even though insurers’ actual fixed-income portfolios 
may differ substantially. Some insurers can therefore benefit excessively from the 
volatility adjustment if the resulting reduction in the best estimate of their liabilities 
exceeds the decline in the value of the relevant assets when credit spreads widen. 
For this reason, the Solvency II revisions change the calculation of the volatility 
adjustment by a) making the applicable risk corrections (RC) more spread-sensitive, and 
b) introducing a credit spread sensitivity ratio (CSSR) to better align the volatility 
adjustment with the insurer’s asset-liability profile. The latter ratio reflects the price 
value of a basis point of the insurer’s bond, securitisation and loan investments versus 
the price value of a basis point of the best estimate of liabilities, with a minimum of 0 
and a maximum of 1. 
Moreover, subject to approval by the supervisory authority, an undertaking-specific 
adjustment (USA) of up to 105% may be applied to align the risk-corrected spread on 
the reference portfolio more closely with that of the insurer’s own portfolio. This must 
not lead to an increase in the applied risk-adjusted spread for more than two 
consecutive quarters. Moreover, when the USA is used, the insurer cannot also increase 
the volatility adjustment through a macro volatility adjustment. 
The macro volatility adjustment replaces the existing country-specific adjustment and 
can be added to the euro volatility adjustment when the relevant conditions are met. It 
aims to mitigate the impact of excessive spread widening in individual euro area 
countries and is based on the difference between the risk-corrected euro spread and the 
risk-corrected spread for the relevant country. 
Volatility adjustment calculations

Subscripts cu = currency, co = country, ma = macro; VA = volatility adjustment, S = spread of the reference 
portfolio, RC = risk correction, RCS = risk-corrected spread of the reference portfolio, CSSR = credit spread 
sensitivity ratio, USA = undertaking-specific adjustment. 
Source: European Commission, ING

The introduction of additional minimum conditions, such as supervisory approval and 
the availability of adequate processes to calculate the volatility adjustment, has also 
facilitated the upward revision of the general application ratio (GAR) from 65% to 85%.

### Page 37

Bank Sector Outlook 2027 October 2026

Primarily through the introduction of the credit spread sensitivity ratio (CSSR), the 
volatility adjustment will better reflect the relationship between each insurer’s asset and 
liability sensitivity to changes in the volatility adjustment. The revision may provide 
modest support for bonds that increase the asset portfolio’s spread sensitivity, as a 
higher CSSR results in a larger volatility adjustment until the ratio reaches its cap of one. 
The revised calculations should also reduce the possibility for some insurers to benefit 
disproportionately from the volatility adjustment when their assets are less spread-
sensitive than the reference portfolio. 
Conclusion 
The Solvency II amendments are unlikely to trigger a material reallocation in favour of 
bank bonds. Securitisations are the most obvious beneficiaries of a more favourable 
capital treatment, while covered bonds and unsecured bank bonds should mainly 
benefit indirectly from lower overall capital requirements for insurers. We therefore 
expect the revisions to only support insurer demand for bank bonds at the margin.

Ngưỡng kỹ thuật

VNINDEX tuần qua hình thành mẫu nến giảm trên đồ thị tuần, nhưng chưa thoái lui quá 1 
nửa cây nến tăng của tuần trước, cùng thanh khoản được tiết giảm, hàm ý nhịp điều chỉnh 
vẫn trong phạm vi cho phép. Trên khung ngày, sau bốn phiên giằng co đầu tuần với thanh 
khoản thấp, chỉ số đã chịu áp lực sụt giảm mạnh hơn trong phiên cuối tuần, chớm xuống 
dưới đường tín hiệu chính giữa dải Bollinger, nhưng cho tín hiệu rút chân ngay sau đó, thể 
hiện áp lực bán tháo đã tạm thời được kiểm soát. Mặc dù rủi ro mở rộng thêm nhịp điều 
chỉnh vẫn còn để ngỏ, tuy nhiên khi xu hướng tăng ngắn hạn đang được bảo lưu, VNINDEX 
được kỳ vọng sẽ cho phản ứng hồi phục trở lại tại các điểm đỡ bên dưới.

(Ghi chú: NĐT có thể dựa theo nhận định xu hướng thị trường để tham khảo khuyến nghị 
hành động tương ứng trong bảng phía trên)

## Document 323: Central banks_ a pause while waiting for more information (2026-10-29)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Central banks_ a pause while waiting for more information (2026-10-29).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-29
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Paris, 30 October 2026

EcoFlash

Central banks: a pause while waiting for more information

As expected, the Fed, the ECB, and the BoJ left their rates unchanged this week. This pause , however, does not mark 
the end of the cycle. The ECB’s tone is becoming more balanced, weighing inflationary risks against tightening financial 
conditions, but one final rate hike in December remains our base case. Across the Atlantic, Kevin Warsh describes an 
environment conducive to further tightening, while remaining cautious about the risk of a credit shock. In Japan, the 
lasting exit from the deflationary cycle is taking shape, and monetary policy adjustment is expected to continue 
gradually. We anticipate two rate hikes by the Fed by January and four by the BoJ by the second half of 2028.

Eurozone: A more balanced tone, but the course remains unchanged. The ECB’s statement marks a shift from September. 
It now explicitly acknowledges both upside risks to inflation (the magnitude and persistence of the energy shock) and downsid e 
pressures (tightening financial conditions). The statement that inflation would  remain “well above target for an extended period” 
no longer appears. In essence, however, there is little change. A rate hike in December remains our base case scenario, widel y 
anticipated and implicitly signaled by Christine Lagarde during the Q&A (“If the current situation is not called into question by the 
next set of economic data, the path is clear”). Lagarde presented the rise in sovereign yields as a global phenomenon that is  
tightening financial conditions; regarding the TPI, she stuck to the usual stance: the ECB will intervene if monetary policy 
transmission were to be hampered “by disorderly movements not justified by fundamentals” . By December, the indirect and 
second-round effects of the energy shock will be closely scrutini sed. From our perspective, September’s producer prices offer 
clearer signals, but the increase remains limited to a few sectors: energy, electricity-intensive industries and electrical equipment, 
driven by IA 1. For now, companies in the Eurozone, particularly in the services sector, appear to be absorbing the shock. The 
European Commission’s survey released this Thursday indicates a rise in the sales price index in October, which remains in line 
with recent months.

United States: The Fed navigates between risks. The FOMC pauses after September’s rate hike and maintains the federal 
funds target at 3.75%-4.0%. However, Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh outlined a landscape conducive to further tightening: stronger 
growth, “rather positive” labor market conditions, and, above all, inflation that is “too high.” As a result, two dissenting votes (out 
of 12) were cast in favor of a 25bps hike.  But the committee also appears to fear the adverse consequences of tightening too 
quickly, which is why it is waiting to “accumulate more information.” AI is increasingly at the heart of monetary policymaking. In 
particular, K. Warsh warned against the risks of an abrupt tightening of interest rate conditions, including for the AI investment 
cycle - which triggered a steepening of the yield curve. He also announced that he would soon (on November 16) attend a hearing 
of the “Productivity and Employment” task force to “refine his views” on the implications of AI for the dual mandate. Governor Lisa 
Cook, who voted for a 25bps hike, had noted ahead of the committee meeting “ongoing inflationary pressures” and a risk that they 
might not “subside quickly”. In addition, K. Warsh announced that there would be fewer meetings resulting in rate decisions: 7 in 
2027 and 6 in 2028, compared with 8 today. The change is expected to take effect starting in the fall of 2027. The meetings freed 
up by this change would allow the committee to focus on “strategic deliberations” and long-term issues.

Japan: A status quo, but a tightening cycle set to continue as the economy emerges from deflation. The Bank of Japan 
decided, in line with our forecasts and the consensus, to keep its policy rate target steady at 1.25% at its October 29-30 meeting. 
One dissenting vote called for a rate hike (25bps), contrasting with the two dovish dissenting votes (opposed to a rate hike) at the 
previous meeting. As in the US, t he decision is justified by the need to further analy se risks to economic activity, prices, and 
financial conditions. Above all, the central bank’s outlook for economic activity and prices marks a turning point: it explicitly states 
that “core inflation has reached a level generally in line with price stability” and that it is “expected to remain there.” Governor Ueda 
refined this by noting that it is now necessary to “anchor” inflation at this level before declaring the target achieved. Furthermore, 
the emphasis on “upside risks to prices” and the fact that an y rise in core inflation is now deemed “undesirable” indicate that 
monetary tightening will continue.

The monetary tightening cycle continues. The next steps are likely to be more significant in the United States, where inflation 
has exceeded the target for nearly six years and coexists with a favorable outlook for growth and employment. In the Eurozone, 
the rise in inflation is primarily due to a supply shock in energy costs (though it is also fueled by demand in the United St ates). It 
continues to have little impact on measures of core inflation, including those tracked by the ECB, such as the H ICP; and 
households’ long-term inflation expectations remain anchored. The degree of monetary tightening also differs. In real terms, the 
ECB’s current rates (the deposit facility rate deflated by the core CPI) hover around 0%, a level that more or less corresponds to 
estimates of the natural rate. The Fed, meanwhile, remains in accommodative territory: the real rate (Fed Funds deflated by Core

1 This is a point underscored by the first PPI releases for September (Spain, Italy).

### Page 2

PCE) is at around 0.8%, 40bps below the committee’s implied long-term level (1.2%). Japan remains an outlier: the rise in rates 
preceded the inflationary shock and, until now, has been more a matter of normalization. It is therefore more extensive and spread 
out over time. The central bank is adjusting to the new inflation regime and remains cautious in its exit from accommodative 
policies. The starting point is significantly lower, with real rates still negative, but the adjustment cycle should ultimately bring it to 
the upper end of the neutral range estimated by the BoJ (1.1% -2.5%). We therefore anticipate one final rate hike by the ECB in 
December, two more hikes by the Fed, first in December and then in January, and four spaced-out hikes by the BoJ through the 
second half of 2028.

Anis Bensaidani   
Economist, United States, Japan 
anis.bensaidani@bnpparibas.com

Guillaume Derrien 
Economist, Europe 
guillaume.a.derrien@bnpparibas.com

## Document 458: US Daily_ Why Has the US Economy Been So Resilient to Higher Rates and Higher Oil Prices_

Source PDF: all report pdf/US Daily_ Why Has the US Economy Been So Resilient to Higher Rates and Higher Oil Prices_.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-09-28
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Abhay Duggirala 
+1(202)585-6760 | 
abhay.duggirala@gs.com 
Goldman Sachs & Co. LLC
US Daily: Why Has the US Economy Been So Resilient to Higher Rates 
and Higher Oil Prices?
28 September 2026 | 10:38AM EDT
We estimate that the economy grew at a 3.4% pace in Q3 and forecast 2.2% n
growth in 2026 overall on a Q4/Q4 basis, close to our estimate of potential GDP 
growth and only 0.3pp below our start-of-the-year forecast before the large 
increases in interest rates and oil prices. Why, we are often asked, has the US 
economy proven so resilient to these shocks? 
While interest rates have risen substantially this year, our financial conditions n
index framework implies that the economic impact should be nearly offset by the 
effects of higher equity prices, narrower credit spreads, and a weaker dollar, 
resulting in only a 0.1pp net hit to 2026 GDP growth. While the net impact of this 
year’s market moves on overall GDP is modest, the effects on some parts of the 
economy—especially the hit to housing—are more meaningful. 
We estimate that the increase in oil and refined product prices this year, together n
with the path our commodities strategists expect through year-end, will subtract 
0.3pp from 2026 growth. The drag comes from consumption as higher energy 
prices erode real purchasing power. While US energy producers were reluctant to 
boost investment when oil prices first rose, the increase in the rig count index in 
recent months points to some offsetting boost to GDP in Q3 and Q4. 
Our analysis suggests that slightly tighter financial conditions and higher oil n
prices should together be roughly a 0.4pp drag on GDP growth. The downgrade 
we have made to our forecast for the year, in part based on incoming data, is only 
slightly smaller than that, with the slight outperformance coming largely from 
consumer spending. This suggests that the economy’s resilience is less puzzling 
than it might appear at first.

### Page 2

Why Has the US Economy Been So Resilient to Higher Rates and Higher Oil Prices?

We now estimate that the economy grew at a 3.4% pace in Q3 and forecast 2.2% growth 
in 2026 overall on a Q4/Q4 basis, close to our 2.3% estimate of potential GDP growth 
and only 0.3pp below our start-of-the-year forecast when we did not expect large 
increases in interest rates and oil prices. In today’s Daily, we ask why the US economy has 
proven so resilient to these surprises. 
Part of the answer is that financial conditions have changed little on net since January, as 
the substantial rise in interest rates has been nearly offset by the effects of higher equity 
prices, narrower credit spreads, and a weaker dollar (Exhibit 1, left). Our estimated GDP 
growth impulse from the FCI is therefore little changed from the start of the year (Exhibit 
1, right), implying only a 0.1pp hit to 2026 Q4/Q4 GDP growth.

While the net impact of this year’s market moves on overall GDP is modest, the effects 
on some components of GDP are more meaningful. We use the Fed’s macroeconomic 
model FRB/US to estimate how the particular combination of market moves this year 
should affect each component of GDP . We find a meaningful hit from higher rates to 
residential investment, offset by slight boosts to consumption and business investment 
(Exhibit 2).

### Page 3

What about the effect of higher oil and refined products prices? Our rule of thumb 
implies that a sustained $10/barrel increase in oil prices lowers GDP growth by just over 
0.1pp through the consumption channel as higher energy costs erode real purchasing 
power. Applied to the path oil prices have taken this year, the unusual widening in 
refined product spreads, and our commodities strategists’ forecasts for prices through 
year-end, the rule of thumb implies a 0.4pp drag on GDP growth. 
While US oil producers were initially reluctant to respond to higher prices by increasing 
capex because of the uncertainty of the war with Iran and the outlook for oil prices, the 
increase in the rig count index in Q3 (Exhibit 3) points to a pickup in energy capex more 
recently. We estimate that the consumption drag and capex boost will net out to a 0.3pp 
hit to GDP growth from the oil shock. Two alternative approaches, FRB/US simulations 
and a model estimated using exogenous oil shocks, deliver similar estimates on average.1 
1 We use the series of exogenous oil shocks from Känzig (2021), “The Macroeconomic Effects of Oil Supply 
News: Evidence from OPEC Announcements” to estimate the effects of the current oil shock on GDP growth.

Sensitive to Higher Interest Rates

conditions were unchanged from the start of the year.

### Page 4

Our analysis suggests that slightly tighter financial conditions and higher oil prices 
should together be roughly a 0.4pp drag on GDP growth. The downgrade we have made 
to our forecast for the year, in part based on incoming data, is only slightly smaller than 
that, suggesting that the puzzle of economic resilience is smaller than it might appear at 
first. 
The economy’s slight outperformance relative to what we might have expected largely 
comes from consumer spending. At the start of the year, we forecasted consumption 
growth of 2.4% in 2026 Q4/Q4. Under the market and commodity price moves that 
actually materialized, our oil shock model implies a 0.6pp hit to that forecast, partially 
offset by a 0.2pp boost from higher equity prices in our wealth effects model, netting to 
an expected 2.0% growth in consumption this year. Against that, we now estimate—in 
part based on the surprisingly solid incoming data in recent months—that consumption 
will grow 2.2%, a modest upside surprise of 0.2pp. 
Abhay Duggirala

## Document 482: “价格不敏感”的买家碰上了“价格不敏感”的卖家！高盛解读：黄金为何跌破4000，为何后续更可能下跌

Source PDF: all report pdf/“价格不敏感”的买家碰上了“价格不敏感”的卖家！高盛解读：黄金为何跌破4000，为何后续更可能下跌.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-11-02
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

⻩⾦正在经历⼀场教科书式的失灵 —— 实际利率下⾏、股市承压、地缘危机三重利好同时
在场，⾦价却跌破 4000 美元，较历史⾼点累计回落 30% 。⾼盛揭⽰，幕后推⼿并⾮对冲基
⾦，⽽是主权实体为应对流动性需求被迫抛售⻩⾦储备，五周内 195 亿美元砸出，速度远超
央⾏每⽉ 85 吨的接盘节奏。头⼨尚未出清， 3850 美元或将成为下⼀道防线。
摘要：
⻩⾦正在经历⼀场教科书式的失灵。所有应当推动⾦价上涨的宏观因素同时出现，⾦价却反向
下跌，并于上周五跌破 4000 美元关键整数关⼝。⾼盛的最新分析揭⽰了这场异常抛售背后的真
实推⼿，并警告市场结构尚未出清，下⾏⻛险仍未消除。
上周五， 12 ⽉⻩⾦期货收盘报 3898.90 美元 / 盎司，单⽇跌幅 3.3% ，现货⾦价触及 2025 年 10 ⽉以
来最低⽔平，较 1 ⽉份接近 5600 美元的历史⾼点累计回落约 30% 。⾼盛⼤宗商品团队在其每周报
告中指出，此次跌破 4000 美元发⽣在市场⾯临周末地缘⻛险、整体⻛险偏好收缩的背景下，当

⽇ CME 成交量较前⼀⽇激增 80% ， GLD ETF 单⽇成交约 40 亿美元，位列当⽇全市场 ETF 成交额
第七位。
⾼盛分析师 Robert Quinn 在告中指出，⾃ 9 ⽉ 22 ⽇以来，市场已累计抛售⻩⾦期货头⼨ 195 亿美
元，且抛售节奏持续加快。值得注意的是，此次抛售的主⼒并⾮通常被归咎的对冲基⾦，⽽是
来⾃ " 其他 " 类别及不可报告类别的资⾦，两者合计占总抛售量的约 95% 。与此同时，期权市场的
看跌情绪已升⾄⼀年来的 85 百分位⾼位，市场正在为进⼀步下跌积极布局保护。
这场抛售的核⼼⽭盾在于：⽀撑⾦价的结构性买盘 —— 各国央⾏ —— 依然存在且持续⼊场，但
其买⼊节奏远慢于当前的抛售速度。" 价格不敏感 " 的买家碰上了同样 " 价格不敏感 " 的卖家：央⾏
不在乎价格⾼低照单全收，⽽主权国家等⼤型持仓⽅为满⾜流动性需求，同样不计价格地抛出
⻩⾦这⼀最具流动性的储备资产。两股⼒量的博弈中，速度更快的⼀⽅暂时占据上⻛。
抛售规模与节奏：连续五周、 195 亿美元

据⾼盛对 CFTC 持仓报告的分析，在 10 ⽉ 20 ⽇⾄ 27 ⽇的观察窗⼝内，投机性⻩⾦抛售进⼀步提
速。管理型资⾦、其他类别及不可报告类别合计抛售 80 亿美元，为连续第五周录得看跌资⾦流
出，五周累计抛售规模达 195 亿美元，其中平仓 52 亿美元，新建空头 28 亿美元。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

从规模来看，以约 4000 美元 / 盎司计算， 195 亿美元对应约 490 万盎司，折合约 150 吨⻩⾦。⾼盛
⾃⾝的央⾏购⾦追踪模型显⽰，官⽅部⻔当前购⾦节奏约为每⽉ 85 吨。换⾔之，投机性资⾦在
五周内的抛售量，相当于央⾏近两个⽉的购⾦总量。
更值得关注的是抛售主体的构成。对冲基⾦（管理型资⾦）在此轮抛售中⼏乎缺席，" 其他 " 类别
及不可报告类别合计贡献了约 95% 的抛售量，其中 " 其他 " 类别的多头平仓规模达 40 亿美元，是
最主要的抛售来源。CFTC 定义下的 " 其他 " 类别涵盖家族办公室、⾃营交易商及其他⾮对冲基⾦
性质的⼤型账⼾，属于换⼿率较低、持仓更具粘性的 " 慢钱 " 。
宏观信号全部失灵：主权流动性管理的阴影
此轮⾦价下跌的异常之处在于，所有传统利多因素均已到位。在观察窗⼝内，美国 10 年期实际
利率下⾏ 11 个基点，股市承压，中东地缘局势持续紧张 —— 这三项历来是⻩⾦最可靠的上涨催
化剂。然⽽⾦价在此期间仍下跌 1.6% 。
⾼盛的 Robert Quinn 将部分原因归结为 " 对主权流动性管理的担忧 " 以及美元⾛强。布伦特原油同

期上涨 4.1% ，美元指数上涨 0.6% 。⾼盛指出， " 其他 " 类别多头头⼨与美元及布伦特原油的滚动 6
个⽉相关性均呈 " 适度负相关 "—— 即油价和美元⾛强时，这类多头倾向于平仓离场。
" 主权流动性管理 " 这⼀表述指向⼀个令市场不安的可能性：随着布伦特原油持续上涨、地区冲突
延续，部分⽯油进⼝国乃⾄⽯油出⼝国主权实体可能⽐持有⻩⾦更需要美元，⽽⻩⾦恰恰是其
储备资产中流动性最强的品种。⾼盛同时注意到，近期有⼤量⻩⾦实物流向伦敦 —— ⽽流⼊伦
敦的⾦条，正是可以在伦敦市场出售的⾦条。
头⼨尚未出清：下⾏⻛险仍在
⾼盛明确表⽰，此轮抛售尚未结束。截⾄ 10 ⽉ 27 ⽇， " 其他 " 类别的多头头⼨在名义规模和占未
平仓合约⽐例两个维度上，均仍⾼于两年中位数⽔平。该类别多头头⼨占未平仓合约⽐例在 10
⽉中旬⼀度攀升⾄约 20% ，为⾼盛⼀年期图表中的最⾼值，尽管经历近期抛售后已回落⾄约
18% ，但仍远⾼于 2 ⽉份约 11%-12% 的低点。

### Page 3

COT 数据的截⽌⽇期为 10 ⽉ 27 ⽇，此后市场进⼀步恶化。⾼盛数据显⽰， 10 ⽉ 27 ⽇⾄ 30 ⽇，⾦
价再度下跌 4.3% ，布伦特原油进⼀步上涨 3.7% 成为主要压制因素，美元和实际利率的回落已⽆
法为⾦价提供⽀撑。
在 ETF 层⾯，截⾄上周四， GLD 的持仓量仍距历史⾼点仅约 1% 。⾼盛测算，即便上周五的 ETF
资⾦流出全部来⾃减仓，也不过约 100 万盎司，⽽ GLD 总持仓超过 1 亿盎司。这意味着潜在的抛
售压⼒仍相当可观。
期权市场：看跌情绪升⾄年内⾼位
期权市场的信号同样不容乐观。据 Quinn 报告，⻩⾦ 3 个⽉隐含波动率和经标准化处理的 25 delta
看跌 / 看涨偏斜度均出现上升，后者已达到⼀年期排名的 85 百分位。
⾼盛数据显⽰，偏斜度从 9 ⽉份的约 -10 ⼤幅攀升⾄约 +6 ， 3 个⽉隐含波动率从 2026 年低点附近
的 19% 回升⾄ 21% 以上。上周五单⽇隐含波动率跳升 3.5 个波动率点，两周⾼频实现波动率已运
⾏⾄ 18.5 。六周前的 9 ⽉份，市场争相买⼊看涨期权；如今，看跌期权的溢价已升⾄ 85 百分位⾼

位。
对此，⾼盛⼤宗商品交易台表⽰，⽬前 " 期权和 delta ⻛险敞⼝有限 " ，正在等待重新⼊场的信号，
尚未从多头⽅向部署更多⻛险。
下⼀个⽀撑位与结构性多头的⻓期逻辑
⾼盛⼤宗商品交易台将 3850 美元视为下⼀个关键⽀撑位，但同时承认这是⼀个 " 移动⽬标 " ，并
坦⾔此前预期 3950 美元能够守住的判断已被证伪。 3850 美元距当前期货价格 3898.90 美元不⾜
1.5% ，同时也低于⾼盛商品研究团队上⽉在 " 鹰派美联储下⾏情景 " 中设定的 4070 美元近期底部
—— 彼时该团队认为持续的央⾏购⾦将逐步抬⾼这⼀底部。
⽀撑⾦价的结构性逻辑并未改变：各国央⾏仍在持续购⾦，去美元化趋势未有逆转，⾼盛研究
团队维持⻩⾦ 2027 年底⽬标价 5400 美元不变，并认为官⽅购⾦将是实现这⼀⽬标的主要驱动
⼒。

然⽽，结构性买盘的节奏偏慢，⽽当前的抛售速度更快。在 " 其他 " 类别多头头⼨仍⾼于两年中位
数、期权偏斜度处于⾼位、交易台按兵不动、美联储 12 ⽉加息概率被市场定价在约 75% 的背景
下，⾼盛认为阻⼒最⼩的⽅向仍是先⾏下探 —— 测试 3850 美元⽀撑，并存在跌破的可能 —— 之
后才是央⾏⼊场逢低买⼊的机会窗⼝。尤其是若油价在⼤宗商品团队所关注的 " 中期选举后升级
窗⼝ " 内持续上涨，这⼀路径将更为清晰。

《醫療器械喜憂參半，但必要性手術需求仍佳》
近月醫療服務設備股為健護中股價表現較差 ，主因：

擴大目標市場 ，但必要醫療手術需求健康 ，心血管
類手術下半年仍成長，醫療器材基本面喜憂參半。

加，低於4星級業者面臨品質獎金減少與長期獲利承
壓風險。另無意外法案獨立爭議解決 (IDR)仲裁成本
持續推升商業保險理賠支出 ，增加2027年雇主團體
及ACA市場保費定價壓力 。管理式醫療業者今年展

現利潤優先於市占率、砍不賺錢計畫/人數拉抬利潤
率，但明年若保費調漲幅度不及醫療成本增速 ，恐
推升醫療損失率 、壓縮承保利潤 。後續獲利關鍵在

間的長期獲利表現可能進一步分化。
